## Acts 17:16–34 (id: 9408)

In this chapter, we see Paul presented as a model witness for Christ, engaging the thinkers of his day and challenging them with the Christian message. Paul quoted writers his audience would be familiar with and showed the relevance of the gospel by dialoguing with them, critiquing their assumptions, and offering Jesus as a constructive alternative (see [Col 1:28](https://ref.ly/Col1:28)). Paul reminded these proud intellectuals that there is a living God to whom all human beings are answerable; that they will be judged by him through Jesus, whom God raised from the dead; and that they should therefore repent and put their faith in Jesus.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 17:16-34

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Areopagus (ID: `place:Areopagus`); Athens (ID: `place:Athens`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Worship (ID: `keyterm:Worship`); Resurrection (ID: `keyterm:Resurrection`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`); Areopagites (ID: `group:Areopagus`); Dionysius (ID: `person:Dionysius`); Stoics (ID: `group:Stoic`); Epicureans (ID: `group:Epicurean`); Damaris (ID: `person:Damaris`); Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`); Athenians (ID: `group:Athens`); Divine (ID: `keyterm:Divine`); Proof (ID: `keyterm:Proof`); Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`); Good News (ID: `keyterm:GoodNews`); Serve (ID: `keyterm:Serve`); Spirit (ID: `keyterm:Spirit.2`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`); Bring to the Attention Of (ID: `keyterm:BringToTheAttentionOf`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Repent (ID: `keyterm:Repent`); Object of Worship (ID: `keyterm:ObjectOfWorship`); Sky (ID: `keyterm:Heaven`); Name (ID: `keyterm:Name`); Obligated (ID: `keyterm:Ought`); Demons (ID: `deity:Demons`); Stone Altar (ID: `realia:StoneAltar`); Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`); Synagogue (ID: `realia:Synagogue`)
## Acts 17:18 (id: 9409)

Epicurean . . . philosophers: Epicureanism was a popular school of Greek philosophy, founded by Epicurus (341–270 BC). Epicureans believed that the principal aim of life was to secure happiness. They thought of pleasure not in terms of sensual indulgence, as their critics charged, but in terms of tranquility. Their contemporaries often called them atheists; in their view, there were no gods to fear, and death simply marked the end of human existence. They sought their security in organized communities where they could live in contentment apart from society. • Stoic philosophers: Stoicism was founded by Zeno of Citium (335–263 BC) and became the most influential philosophy in the Greco\-Roman world. It viewed the universe as permeated by Reason (sometimes referred to as God or Providence). Stoicism saw divine Reason as expressed in human reason and held that as humans made progress, they could advance from ignorance (the source of vice) to true knowledge (the source of virtue). They developed extensive lists of virtues and vices and produced detailed household codes to guide family behavior. Paul’s teaching resembles that of the Stoics in his use of household codes and lists of virtues and vices ([Gal 5:19–23](https://ref.ly/Gal5:19-Gal5:23); [Eph 5:22–33](https://ref.ly/Eph5:22-Eph5:33); [Col 3:18–4:1](https://ref.ly/Col3:18-Col4:1); [1 Tim 3:1–13](https://ref.ly/1Tim3:1-1Tim3:13); [5:1–6:1](https://ref.ly/1Tim5:1-1Tim5:6)). However, Paul’s message of Good News—focusing on the life, death, and resurrection of Jesus Christ—was strange and foreign to these Greek philosophers. • The air of superiority with which they addressed Paul as this babbler indicates their arrogance.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 17:18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Epicureans (ID: `group:Epicurean`); Stoics (ID: `group:Stoic`); Good News (ID: `keyterm:GoodNews`); Resurrection (ID: `keyterm:Resurrection`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Demons (ID: `deity:Demons`)
## Acts 17:22–31 (id: 9410)

Paul’s remarkable sermon in Athens reveals his versatility in preaching the Good News ([Rom 11:14](https://ref.ly/Rom11:14); [1 Cor 9:19–23](https://ref.ly/1Cor9:19-1Cor9:23); [10:33](https://ref.ly/1Cor10:33); cp. [Acts 16:3](https://ref.ly/Acts16:3); [17:2–3](https://ref.ly/Acts17:2-Acts17:3); [21:20–26](https://ref.ly/Acts21:20-Acts21:26)). While Paul’s Greek audience did not know the Scriptures or have a tradition of monotheism as the Jews did, they did have a rich intellectual heritage. So Paul established a point of contact on the basis of an Athenian inscription to an Unknown God. He then explained God’s nature as the Creator ([17:22–29](https://ref.ly/Acts17:22-Acts17:29)), followed by God’s purpose as the Redeemer ([17:30–31](https://ref.ly/Acts17:30-Acts17:31)). • As he did elsewhere in his own writings (see [1 Cor 15:33](https://ref.ly/1Cor15:33); [Titus 1:12](https://ref.ly/Titus1:12)), Paul made use of the Greek poets. There are several points of connection in this sermon with the *Hymn to Zeus* by Cleanthes (about 315–240 BC). Paul was probably quoting Epimenides, who had declared about God, “In him we live and move and exist” (Epimenides, *Cretica,* about 600 BC). Paul also cited Aratus, a Stoic poet from Cilicia (about 315–240 BC), who had commented, “We are his offspring” (Aratus, *Phaenomena* 5\). These allusions to their own poets established connections with his audience. Paul was then able to present the singular nature of God ([Acts 17:29](https://ref.ly/Acts17:29)), and he stressed the coming judgment through Jesus Christ, whom God had raised from the dead. This message, while touching on Greek culture and philosophy, had a clear focus on Christ that presented the challenge of Christ to a cultured and intellectual but idolatrous people.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 17:22-31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`); Areopagus (ID: `place:Areopagus`); Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`); Worship (ID: `keyterm:Worship`); Divine (ID: `keyterm:Divine`); Proof (ID: `keyterm:Proof`); Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`); Serve (ID: `keyterm:Serve`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Repent (ID: `keyterm:Repent`); Athens (ID: `place:Athens`); Object of Worship (ID: `keyterm:ObjectOfWorship`); Sky (ID: `keyterm:Heaven`); Obligated (ID: `keyterm:Ought`); Image (ID: `realia:Image`); Stone Altar (ID: `realia:StoneAltar`); Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`)
## Acts 17:30 (id: 9411)

The idea of people’s ignorance is carefully discussed both in the Old Testament law ([Lev 4:2](https://ref.ly/Lev4:2), [22](https://ref.ly/Lev4:22), [27](https://ref.ly/Lev4:27); [5:15](https://ref.ly/Lev5:15), [17](https://ref.ly/Lev5:17); [Num 15:25](https://ref.ly/Num15:25), [27](https://ref.ly/Num15:27)) and in the New Testament ([Eph 4:18](https://ref.ly/Eph4:18); [1 Pet 1:14](https://ref.ly/1Pet1:14); [2:15](https://ref.ly/1Pet2:15); [2 Pet 3:5](https://ref.ly/2Pet3:5), [8](https://ref.ly/2Pet3:8)). Paul was particularly fervent about combatting ignorance (see [Rom 10:13–15](https://ref.ly/Rom10:13-Rom10:15); [11:25](https://ref.ly/Rom11:25); [1 Cor 10:1](https://ref.ly/1Cor10:1); [12:1](https://ref.ly/1Cor12:1); [2 Cor 1:8](https://ref.ly/2Cor1:8); [1 Thes 4:13](https://ref.ly/1Thess4:13)). The message of Good News overcomes ignorance and summons all who hear it to repent of their sins and turn to God (see [Acts 2:38](https://ref.ly/Acts2:38)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 17:30

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Repent (ID: `keyterm:Repent`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)
## Acts 17:32 (id: 9412)

The Athenians listened carefully until Paul spoke of the resurrection of the dead ([17:31](https://ref.ly/Acts17:31)); at that point some laughed or mocked, for the notion of resurrection was foolish to Greek ears (see [1 Cor 15:12–19](https://ref.ly/1Cor15:12-1Cor15:19)). Similarly, the Jews in Jerusalem later listened carefully to Paul until he mentioned God’s acceptance of the Gentiles ([Acts 22:22](https://ref.ly/Acts22:22)). These are examples of how the message of Good News can offend people because of their prejudices (see [1 Cor 1:20–25](https://ref.ly/1Cor1:20-1Cor1:25)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 17:32

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Resurrection (ID: `keyterm:Resurrection`)
## Acts 17:34 (id: 9413)

some joined him and became believers: Paul’s sermon was not without positive response. Two prominent converts are mentioned: Dionysius and Damaris. ## Acts 18:1 (id: 9423)

In Roman times, Greek political power in Achaia resided in Corinth*,* a prominent city\-state and major commercial city. Corinth was notorious for its prostitution, immorality, and drunkenness (see 1 Corinthians Book Introduction, “Setting”). Paul invested much time and effort establishing a Christian community in Corinth (see study note on Acts 16:8).

Paul spent eighteen fruitful months preaching and teaching in Corinth*,* first in the synagogue and then next door in the house of Titius Justus ([18:1–11](https://ref.ly/Acts18:1-Acts18:11)). Then, in court, Paul won a significant victory over his enemies ([18:12–17](https://ref.ly/Acts18:12-Acts18:17)).

## Acts 18:2–3 (id: 9424)

The edict of Claudius Caesar that had deported all Jews from Rome around AD 49 is mentioned by the Roman historian Suetonius (*Life of Claudius* 25\). • Aquila and Priscilla became an outstanding husband\-and\-wife team in the early church. They earned their living as tentmakers, as did Paul, who probably lived and worked with them during his year and a half in Corinth ([18:11](https://ref.ly/Acts18:11); see [Rom 16:3–4](https://ref.ly/Rom16:3-Rom16:4); [1 Cor 16:19](https://ref.ly/1Cor16:19)). • just as he was: Paul would have been trained as a tentmaker as a young man. It was Jewish custom to provide sons with a manual trade, including young men who intended to become rabbis or other professionals.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 18:2-3

Cenchrea was a seaport located on the Aegean Sea, roughly five miles (8 km) east of Corinth. ## Acts 18:19–23 (id: 9422)

Paul made a quick stop at Ephesus*,* the most important city in the Roman province of Asia. There he left the others behind*,* including Priscilla and Aquila ([18:26](https://ref.ly/Acts18:26)). He sailed to Judea, landing at Caesarea*,* the headquarters of the Roman forces of occupation. After a visit to the church at Jerusalem*,* Paul returned to Antioch*,* the church that had originally commissioned him. This marked the end of his second missionary journey. In Antioch, he spent a rewarding time of reporting what God had done through him and his colleagues, sharing the excitement and challenges of their work with the home church. Then after . . . some time*,* Paul began his third missionary journey. He went by land rather than by sea, traveling through Galatia and Phrygia and revisiting believers whom he had led to faith in Christ on his previous trips. It was important to him that these young converts not be left to founder and shipwreck their faith (see [Eph 6:10–20](https://ref.ly/Eph6:10-Eph6:20); [1 Tim 1:18–20](https://ref.ly/1Tim1:18-1Tim1:20); [2 Tim 1:15](https://ref.ly/2Tim1:15); [4:10](https://ref.ly/2Tim4:10)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 18:19-23

## Acts 18:24–26 (id: 9428)

Alexandria*,* the second\-largest city in the Roman Empire, was famous for its rhetorical tradition and the philosophical work of Philo. Apollos was an eloquent speaker with an excellent knowledge of the Scriptures (the Old Testament). His knowledge about Jesus and the Holy Spirit was inadequate, though. He did not understand that believers could experience and enjoy the power of the Holy Spirit as a present energizing reality. Fortunately, Priscilla and Aquila took him aside and corrected his spiritual understanding.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 18:24-26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); The Way (ID: `keyterm:TheWay`); Apollos (ID: `person:Apollos`); Alexandria (ID: `place:Alexandria`); Alexandrians (ID: `group:Alexandria`); Aquila (ID: `person:Aquila`); Ephesus (ID: `place:Ephesus`); John (ID: `person:John`); Name (ID: `keyterm:Name`); Priscilla (ID: `person:Priscilla`); Baptism (ID: `keyterm:Baptism`); Written (ID: `keyterm:Scripture`); Spirit (ID: `keyterm:Spirit.2`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Synagogue (ID: `realia:Synagogue`)
## Acts 19:1–7 (id: 9432)

Paul traveled to Ephesus after Apollos had left. Subsequently, Apollos returned to Ephesus while Paul was still working there ([1 Cor 16:12](https://ref.ly/1Cor16:12)). The two men had different personalities, gifts, and roles, but God worked through both of them. • Some believers in Ephesus still had an inadequate understanding of the Christian faith (cp. [Acts 18:26](https://ref.ly/Acts18:26)) and did not receive the Holy Spirit when they believed. They had accepted John’s baptism*,* which called for repentance from sin*,* but had not received Christian baptism, which included the gift of the Holy Spirit. When Paul had instructed them further, they were able to receive Christian baptism ([19:5](https://ref.ly/Acts19:5)) and the Holy Spirit ([19:6](https://ref.ly/Acts19:6)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 19:1-7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Baptism (ID: `keyterm:Baptism`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`); John (ID: `person:John`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Holy Spirit (ID: `deity:HolySpirit`); Ephesus (ID: `place:Ephesus`); Apollos (ID: `person:Apollos`); Name (ID: `keyterm:Name`); Corinth (ID: `place:Corinth`); Be a Disciple (ID: `keyterm:Disciple`); Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`); Repent (ID: `keyterm:Repent`)
## Acts 19:6 (id: 9448)

When Paul laid his hands on them they received the Holy Spirit, spoke in other tongues and prophesied*,* thereby experiencing the same filling with God’s power and presence that the disciples had received on the day of Pentecost ([2:4](https://ref.ly/Acts2:4), [11](https://ref.ly/Acts2:11)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 19:6

[Matt 12:27](https://ref.ly/Matt12:27); [Mark 9:38–39](https://ref.ly/Mark9:38-Mark9:39); [Luke 9:49–50](https://ref.ly/Luke9:49-Luke9:50); [11:19](https://ref.ly/Luke11:19)).

## Acts 19:17–19 (id: 9436)

The contrast between Paul and the Jewish exorcists was not lost on the people of Ephesus, Jews and Greeks alike. The resulting fear caused the inhabitants to honor and respect the name of the Lord Jesus. It led many to confess and forsake their sinful practices*,* including sorcery.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 19:17-19

[2:43–47](https://ref.ly/Acts2:43-Acts2:47); [5:14](https://ref.ly/Acts5:14); [6:7](https://ref.ly/Acts6:7); [9:31](https://ref.ly/Acts9:31); [12:24](https://ref.ly/Acts12:24); [16:5](https://ref.ly/Acts16:5); see [Rom 1:16](https://ref.ly/Rom1:16)). ## Acts 19:21–22 (id: 9438)

felt compelled by the Spirit: No doubt Paul was troubled by what he had heard about the situation in Corinth (see 1 Corinthians Book Introduction). Instead of going immediately himself, he sent . . . Timothy*,* who was carrying a letter, 1 Corinthians, from Paul to the Corinthians ([1 Cor 4:17](https://ref.ly/1Cor4:17); [16:10–11](https://ref.ly/1Cor16:10-1Cor16:11)). Paul himself did go over to Macedonia and Achaia not long afterward ([Acts 20:1–3](https://ref.ly/Acts20:1-Acts20:3)). • “I must go on to Rome!”: Perhaps compelled by his proven strategy (see study note on 16:8), Paul wanted to proclaim the Good News in the most significant city in the world.

## Acts 19:23–41 (id: 9439)

The patron deity of Ephesus was the Greek goddess Artemis. Her birthplace was believed to be Ephesus, so Ephesus was the official guardian of the temple. Twice annually, elaborate festivals were held in her honor with athletic, musical, and theatrical celebrations that included singing Great is Artemis of the Ephesians! The temple of Artemis at Ephesus was one of the seven wonders of the ancient world. Conversions to Christianity clearly damaged the worship of Artemis and the associated economic activity, but it became clear that Paul and his associates had committed no crime.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 19:23-41

## Acts 19:31 (id: 9445)

Some of the officials of the province (Greek Asiarchai, “rulers of Asia”): The “Asiarchs” were the appointed leaders in the Roman province of Asia. They served as civic benefactors and usually championed the emperor cult. Some of these officials in high places were friends of Paul—Luke highlights that Christianity was attractive to people of high standing in society.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 19:31

## Acts 20:1–2 (id: 9463)

Paul traveled to Macedonia*,* where he encouraged the believers in all the towns*,* including Thessalonica, Philippi, and Berea. He also continued to gather the offering for the needy in Jerusalem (see [Rom 15:25–28](https://ref.ly/Rom15:25-Rom15:28); [2 Cor 8:1–9:15](https://ref.ly/2Cor8:1-2Cor9:15)). Titus met Paul in Macedonia with a report from Corinth, which prompted Paul to write 2 Corinthians and send Titus back carrying it ([2 Cor 7:5–7](https://ref.ly/2Cor7:5-2Cor7:7); [8:6](https://ref.ly/2Cor8:6)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 20:1-2

## Acts 20:7–12 (id: 9477)

On the first day of the week the early church commemorated Jesus’ resurrection (see [Mark 16:9](https://ref.ly/Mark16:9); cp. [John 20:19](https://ref.ly/John20:19); [Rev 1:10](https://ref.ly/Rev1:10)). • This was Paul’s last visit to Troas, which included gathering to share in the Lord’s Supper. This meal probably included both communion and a common meal (cp. [Acts 2:42](https://ref.ly/Acts2:42), [46](https://ref.ly/Acts2:46); [Jude 1:12](https://ref.ly/Jude1:12)). • The remarkable feature of this gathering was the understated miraculous restoration of Eutychus*,* the young man who fell asleep on a windowsill and dropped three stories to his death (cp. [Acts 9:36–41](https://ref.ly/Acts9:36-Acts9:41)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 20:7-12

## Acts 20:18–38 (id: 9467)

Paul’s address to the elders of the church of Ephesus is a testimony regarding his life and ministry in Ephesus, calling for similar dedication from the leaders who would carry on the ministry. The sermon highlights Paul’s integrity and pastoral care ([20:18–21](https://ref.ly/Acts20:18-Acts20:21), [26](https://ref.ly/Acts20:26), [31](https://ref.ly/Acts20:31)), speaks about the future ([20:22–23](https://ref.ly/Acts20:22-Acts20:23), [25](https://ref.ly/Acts20:25), [29–30](https://ref.ly/Acts20:29-Acts20:30)), warns against false teachers ([20:29–30](https://ref.ly/Acts20:29-Acts20:30)), and exhorts the elders to be watchful and faithful ([20:28](https://ref.ly/Acts20:28), [31](https://ref.ly/Acts20:31)). Paul modeled sacrificial, conscientious, servant leadership.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 20:18-38

Paul refers to the church as God’s people and God’s flock (cp. [1 Pet 2:25](https://ref.ly/1Pet2:25); [5:2](https://ref.ly/1Pet5:2), [4](https://ref.ly/1Pet5:4)). Elsewhere the church is called *the body of Christ* ([1 Cor 12:27](https://ref.ly/1Cor12:27); [Eph 1:23](https://ref.ly/Eph1:23); [4:12](https://ref.ly/Eph4:12); [Col 1:24](https://ref.ly/Col1:24)); the *bride* of Christ ([2 Cor 11:2](https://ref.ly/2Cor11:2); [Rev 19:7](https://ref.ly/Rev19:7); see [Eph 5:25–32](https://ref.ly/Eph5:25-Eph5:32)); *the temple of the living God* ([1 Cor 3:16](https://ref.ly/1Cor3:16); [2 Cor 6:16](https://ref.ly/2Cor6:16)); *a chosen people*, *royal priests, a holy nation, God’s very own possession* ([1 Pet 2:9](https://ref.ly/1Pet2:9)); and *God’s field*, *God’s building* ([1 Cor 3:9](https://ref.ly/1Cor3:9)). • Paul expected the leaders to feed and shepherd the church over which the Holy Spirit had appointed them as leaders (see [1 Tim 3:1–7](https://ref.ly/1Tim3:1-1Tim3:7); [Titus 1:5–7](https://ref.ly/Titus1:5-Titus1:7); [1 Pet 5:1–4](https://ref.ly/1Pet5:1-1Pet5:4); cp. [Acts 6:2–4](https://ref.ly/Acts6:2-Acts6:4)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 20:28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Blood (ID: `keyterm:Blood`); Holy Spirit (ID: `deity:HolySpirit`); Church (ID: `keyterm:Church`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Acts 21:1 (id: 9482)

Cos was an island in the Aegean Sea with a major trade port. • Rhodes is a large Aegean island that featured the Colossus, a huge statue 100 feet (30 meters) tall that once stood at the entrance to the city. In Paul’s time, the statue lay where it had fallen during an earthquake over 200 years earlier; it would not be removed for another 600 years. • Patara was the major port of Lycia, located on the coast opposite Rhodes.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cos (ID: `place:Cos`); Rhodes (ID: `place:Rhodes`); Patara (ID: `place:Patara`)

## Acts 21:4–6 (id: 9491)

The local believers at Tyre gave Paul a touching farewell that reveals deep Christian fellowship. • prophesied through the Holy Spirit that Paul should not go on to Jerusalem: The believers at Tyre clearly foresaw the danger in Paul’s visiting Jerusalem and out of brotherly concern tried to dissuade him. Paul willingly accepted the risks in order to fulfill his apostolic mandate (cp. [Phil 3:7–10](https://ref.ly/Phil3:7-Phil3:10)). See also study note on Acts 21:11–14.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:4-6

Despite Agabus’s prediction of suffering and the believers’ begging Paul not to go to Jerusalem, Paul was resolutely ready even to die for the sake of the Lord Jesus. Luke stresses Paul’s courage, determination, and heroism as a Christian missionary who would let nothing interfere with his mission. See study note on 21:4–6.

## Acts 21:18–19 (id: 9481)

It was important for James and all the elders of the Jerusalem church to hear of Paul’s successful mission among the Gentiles and for the mother church to continue to endorse this effort ([21:20](https://ref.ly/Acts21:20); see [15:7–21](https://ref.ly/Acts15:7-Acts15:21)). Paul’s report communicated that God had accomplished his purposes among the Gentiles through Paul’s ministry.

## Acts 21:20–25 (id: 9484)

The Jerusalem church leaders urged Paul to strengthen his credibility by demonstrating that he was not teaching Jews to turn their backs on the laws of Moses. At the same time, no attempt was made to force Jewish rules on Gentile converts—those terms had been set previously ([15:22–29](https://ref.ly/Acts15:22-Acts15:29)) and were simply to be honored.

The fortress of Antonia, a large military garrison built by Herod the Great on the northwest corner of the Temple Mount, accommodated the Roman cohort that was stationed there to keep the peace.

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Something Definite (ID: `keyterm:SomethingDefinite`)
## Acts 21:37–40 (id: 9490)

The commander had mistaken Paul for an Egyptian false messiah who had planned to seize power from the Romans around AD 54 (roughly three years earlier; see Josephus, *War* 2\.13\.5\). Paul corrected the mistake, gained permission to speak to the people, and addressed the crowd in Aramaic, the common language of Judea. He gave a strong statement of his faith in Jesus as the Messiah ([Acts 22:1–21](https://ref.ly/Acts22:1-Acts22:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 21:37-40

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Tarsus (ID: `place:Tarsus`); Cilicia (ID: `place:Cilicia`); Tarsians (ID: `group:Tarsus`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`); Military Member (ID: `person:MilitaryMember`); Be Lawful (ID: `keyterm:Lawful`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Step (ID: `realia:Step`)
## Acts 22:1–21 (id: 9495)

Paul’s premier defense of his life and faith before his own people in Jerusalem illustrates his flexibility as a missionary, just as his speech to the Greek philosophers in Athens had done ([17:22–31](https://ref.ly/Acts17:22-Acts17:31); see [1 Cor 9:20–23](https://ref.ly/1Cor9:20-1Cor9:23)). Paul begins by recognizing his kinship with his people, explaining his Jewish background and training under the noted rabbi Gamaliel the Elder and describing his zealous desire to honor God in everything*,* which they shared ([Acts 22:1–3](https://ref.ly/Acts22:1-Acts22:3)). Paul then describes his persecution of Christians ([22:4–5](https://ref.ly/Acts22:4-Acts22:5)), the revelation of Jesus to him on the way to Damascus ([22:6–10](https://ref.ly/Acts22:6-Acts22:10)), and his conversion ([22:11–16](https://ref.ly/Acts22:11-Acts22:16)). Paul ends his speech by describing his conversation with the Lord in the Temple. The Lord had predicted the Jews’ rejection of the message and had sent Paul to the Gentiles ([22:17–21](https://ref.ly/Acts22:17-Acts22:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 22:1-21

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Damascus (ID: `place:Damascus`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Bear Witness (ID: `keyterm:BearWitness`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Law (ID: `keyterm:Law`); Witness (ID: `keyterm:Witness`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Gamaliel (ID: `person:Gamaliel`); Ananias (ID: `person:Ananias.2`); Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`); Baptism (ID: `keyterm:Baptism`); Pray (ID: `keyterm:Pray`); Call Upon (ID: `keyterm:CallUpon`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Cilicia (ID: `place:Cilicia`); The Way (ID: `keyterm:TheWay`); Gentiles (ID: `group:Gentile`); Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); Nazareth (ID: `place:Nazareth`); Tarsus (ID: `place:Tarsus`); Blood (ID: `keyterm:Blood`); Sky (ID: `keyterm:Heaven`); Name (ID: `keyterm:Name`); Stephen (ID: `person:Stephen`); Cloak (ID: `realia:Cloak`); Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`); Synagogue (ID: `realia:Synagogue`); Prison (ID: `realia:Prison`)

While Paul was praying in the Temple*,* he saw a vision of Jesus telling him that the people of Jerusalem would not accept his testimony. The Lord then sent him to the Gentiles. The crowd’s response to this report ([22:22–23](https://ref.ly/Acts22:22-Acts22:23)) proved the point.

## Acts 22:23 (id: 9500)

The crowd’s yelling, throwing off their coats*,* and tossing handfuls of dust into the air were probably ritual responses to perceived blasphemy. They opposed and tried to thwart Paul’s words that suggested the inclusion of Gentiles ([22:21](https://ref.ly/Acts22:21)). Cp. [Luke 4:16–30](https://ref.ly/Luke4:16-Luke4:30).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 22:23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cloak (ID: `realia:Cloak`)
## Acts 22:25–29 (id: 9501)

Paul claimed his status as a Roman citizen at this critical time when he was about to be tortured to make him confess his supposed crime. Roman citizenship was a valuable asset, and claiming it falsely was a capital offense. Its principal benefits were the prohibition of scourging and the right to appeal to the emperor ([25:11](https://ref.ly/Acts25:11)). The commander was frightened at having nearly violated Roman law (cp. [16:35–39](https://ref.ly/Acts16:35-Acts16:39)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 22:25-29

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Military Member (ID: `person:MilitaryMember`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Be Lawful (ID: `keyterm:Lawful`); Strap (ID: `realia:Strap`)
## Acts 22:28 (id: 9502)

it cost me plenty! During the early part of the reign of Emperor Claudius (AD 41–54\), Roman citizenship could be purchased, but it was expensive.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 22:28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Military Member (ID: `person:MilitaryMember`)

## Acts 23:5 (id: 9515)

Why Paul did not recognize the high priest is not known. • Paul replied by quoting [Exod 22:28](https://ref.ly/Exod22:28), acknowledging the respect to which the high priest was entitled by virtue of his office.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`)
## Acts 23:6 (id: 9516)

Paul focused on the key issue in his trial, the hope of resurrection from the dead. His preaching was simply the outworking of that hope and the fact of Jesus’ resurrection, but the message was unacceptable to both groups of Jews because of its implications. Pharisees could not abide the inclusion of the Gentiles apart from circumcision and keeping the law of Moses (cp. [15:5](https://ref.ly/Acts15:5)), but that was what the resurrection of Jesus and the outpouring of the Spirit had provided ([Acts 2:39](https://ref.ly/Acts2:39); [10:34–48](https://ref.ly/Acts10:34-Acts10:48)). Sadducees could not stand the proclamation of the resurrection at all (cp. [4:1–2](https://ref.ly/Acts4:1-Acts4:2)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Pharisees (ID: `group:Pharisee`); Sadducees (ID: `group:Sadducee`); Resurrection (ID: `keyterm:Resurrection`); Hope (ID: `keyterm:Hope`); Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`)
## Acts 23:7–10 (id: 9517)

Paul’s statement ([23:6](https://ref.ly/Acts23:6)) divided the council*,* with the Pharisees taking Paul’s side against the Sadducees. The resulting uproar was so great that the commander rescued Paul and took him back into the fortress of Antonia.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:7-10

## Acts 23:11 (id: 9504)

At this critical juncture, Jesus encouraged Paul to continue as his faithful witness by assuring him that he would go to Rome (see [19:21](https://ref.ly/Acts19:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Cheer Up (ID: `keyterm:CheerUp`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Declare (ID: `keyterm:Declare`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Bear Witness (ID: `keyterm:BearWitness`)
## Acts 23:12–15 (id: 9505)

The plan to kill Paul was desperate, as a group of . . . more than forty took an oath not to eat or drink until they had killed Paul (cp. [1 Sam 14:24–46](https://ref.ly/1Sam14:24-1Sam14:46); [Matt 14:6–11](https://ref.ly/Matt14:6-Matt14:11); see also [Deut 23:21–23](https://ref.ly/Deut23:21-Deut23:23); [Matt 5:33–37](https://ref.ly/Matt5:33-Matt5:37); [Jas 5:12](https://ref.ly/Jas5:12)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:12-15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Decision (ID: `keyterm:Decision`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`)
## Acts 23:16–22 (id: 9506)

Paul’s nephew thwarted the murderous plot by reporting it to one of the Roman officers.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:16-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Ask for (ID: `keyterm:AskFor`); Persuade (ID: `keyterm:Persuade`); Promise (ID: `keyterm:Promise`); Military Member (ID: `person:MilitaryMember`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`)
## Acts 23:23–35 (id: 9508)

A mounted escort took Paul safely to the Roman Governor Felix in Caesarea*,* the Roman headquarters for Judea. There Paul would have greater protection than in Jerusalem. The military operation was executed that night with secret efficiency and maximum security ([23:31](https://ref.ly/Acts23:31)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:23-35

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Caesarea (ID: `place:Caesarea`); Cilicia (ID: `place:Cilicia`); Lysias (ID: `person:Lysias`); Antipatris (ID: `place:Antipatris`); Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Law (ID: `keyterm:Law`); Rescue (ID: `keyterm:Rescue`); Herod (ID: `person:Herod`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`); Palace (ID: `realia:Palace`)
## Acts 23:24 (id: 9509)

Antonius Felix was procurator (Governor) of Judea about AD 52–59, with responsibility for both military and civil affairs. Felix had a bad reputation (see [24:24–27](https://ref.ly/Acts24:24-Acts24:27)) and was eventually recalled to Rome by Nero.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`)
## Acts 23:26–30 (id: 9510)

The letter of Claudius Lysias to Governor Felix is a typical Hellenistic letter, naming the writer and the person addressed, offering a greeting, and stating the business at hand (though there is no parting word of farewell; see study note on 15:29). It summarizes the events that preceded it and explains the action taken; it also suggests that the commander has followed proper Roman judicial procedure. • The title his Excellency was often applied to persons of high social, political, or economic status ([Luke 1:3](https://ref.ly/Luke1:3)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:26-30
* **Associated Articles:** Acts 15:29 (ID: `9373@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Lysias (ID: `person:Lysias`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Law (ID: `keyterm:Law`); Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Rescue (ID: `keyterm:Rescue`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`)
## Acts 23:31 (id: 9512)

Antipatris*,* a city rebuilt by Herod the Great in 9 BC on the Plain of Sharon, was a convenient military control point between Jerusalem and Caesarea.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Antipatris (ID: `place:Antipatris`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`)
## Acts 23:35 (id: 9513)

The governor followed the proper protocol and waited for Paul’s accusers to arrive before granting an official hearing. • Herod’s headquarters was Herod the Great’s palace at Caesarea; it subsequently became the residence of the Roman governors of Judea.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 23:35

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Palace (ID: `realia:Palace`); Herod (ID: `person:Herod`)
## Acts 24:1–4 (id: 9519)

Tertullus presented the case for the prosecution, beginning with the customary speech of praise intended to attract the attention and sympathy of the governor, followed by the statement of charges ([24:5–8](https://ref.ly/Acts24:5-Acts24:8)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:1-4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Tertullus (ID: `person:Tertullus`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Ananias (ID: `person:Ananias.3`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Ask for (ID: `keyterm:AskFor`); Make Peace (ID: `keyterm:Peace`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`)
## Acts 24:1–27 (id: 9518)

Tertullus presented a legal case against Paul in a Roman court on behalf of the high priest ([24:1–9](https://ref.ly/Acts24:1-Acts24:9)). Then Paul cheerfully made his defense and defended his faith ([24:10–21](https://ref.ly/Acts24:10-Acts24:21)), and the governor adjourned the hearing without a decision and left Paul in prison for two years ([24:22–27](https://ref.ly/Acts24:22-Acts24:27)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:1-27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Tertullus (ID: `person:Tertullus`); The Way (ID: `keyterm:TheWay`); Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`); Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`); Resurrection (ID: `keyterm:Resurrection`); Serve (ID: `keyterm:Serve`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`); Hope (ID: `keyterm:Hope`); Lysias (ID: `person:Lysias`); Festus (ID: `person:Festus`); Ananias (ID: `person:Ananias.3`); Drusilla (ID: `person:Drusilla`); Conscience (ID: `keyterm:Conscience`); Bow Down (ID: `keyterm:BowDown`); Asia (ID: `place:Asia`); Pure (ID: `keyterm:Pure`); Ask for (ID: `keyterm:AskFor`); Decision (ID: `keyterm:Decision`); Make Peace (ID: `keyterm:Peace`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Nazareth (ID: `place:Nazareth`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`); Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Judgment (ID: `keyterm:Judgment`); Law (ID: `keyterm:Law`); Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); Priesthood (ID: `keyterm:Priesthood`); Discern (ID: `keyterm:Discern`); Unrighteous Act (ID: `keyterm:UnrighteousAct`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Synagogue (ID: `realia:Synagogue`)
## Acts 24:5 (id: 9531)

Troublemaker (or *agitator*) was a charge of political sedition. A Roman court would have taken this charge very seriously (see [16:21](https://ref.ly/Acts16:21); [17:7](https://ref.ly/Acts17:7); [18:13](https://ref.ly/Acts18:13)). Similar accusations were made against Jesus before Pilate ([Luke 23:2](https://ref.ly/Luke23:2), [5](https://ref.ly/Luke23:5), [14](https://ref.ly/Luke23:14)). • The term cult (or *party*, or *sect*) is used here in a negative sense to put Paul’s religion under a pallor of suspicion if not illegality (see also [Acts 24:14](https://ref.ly/Acts24:14)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Nazareth (ID: `place:Nazareth`)
## Acts 24:6 (id: 9532)

trying to desecrate the Temple: See study note on 21:28–29.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:6
* **Associated Articles:** Acts 21:28-29 (ID: `9486@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Defilement (ID: `keyterm:Defilement`); Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`)
## Acts 24:10–21 (id: 9520)

Paul’s defense was that (1\) he wasn’t in Jerusalem long enough to incite a riot; (2\) none of his accusers had ever seen him stirring up a riot; and (3\) he worshiped in accord with Jewish law and everything written in the prophets.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:10-21

## Acts 24:14 (id: 9521)

the Way: See study note on 9:2. • Paul emphasized his common ground with his Jewish audience, including his worship*,* belief in the Jewish law*,* acceptance of the prophets*,* and hope in the resurrection ([24:14](https://ref.ly/Acts24:14), [15](https://ref.ly/Acts24:15); see [24:21](https://ref.ly/Acts24:21)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:14

## Acts 24:15 (id: 9522)

At the last judgment God will raise both the righteous and the unrighteous. Paul kept this final appointment with God constantly in mind ([24:16](https://ref.ly/Acts24:16)). The fear of the “coming day of judgment” unnerved Felix in a subsequent conversation with Paul ([24:25](https://ref.ly/Acts24:25)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Resurrection (ID: `keyterm:Resurrection`); Hope (ID: `keyterm:Hope`); Be (Or Show Oneself) Just (ID: `keyterm:Just`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)
## Acts 24:16 (id: 9523)

Paul stressed that he had acted with a clear conscience (see [20:27](https://ref.ly/Acts20:27), [33](https://ref.ly/Acts20:33); [23:1](https://ref.ly/Acts23:1); [1 Cor 4:4](https://ref.ly/1Cor4:4); [2 Cor 1:12](https://ref.ly/2Cor1:12); [4:2](https://ref.ly/2Cor4:2); see also [2 Tim 1:3](https://ref.ly/2Tim1:3)). He had not departed from his Jewish heritage ([Acts 24:14](https://ref.ly/Acts24:14)), and he had no fear of God’s judgment ([24:15](https://ref.ly/Acts24:15)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Conscience (ID: `keyterm:Conscience`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)
## Acts 24:17 (id: 9524)

money to aid my people: See study note on 20:1–2; see also [Rom 15:25–29](https://ref.ly/Rom15:25-Rom15:29); [1 Cor 16:1–2](https://ref.ly/1Cor16:1-1Cor16:2); [2 Cor 9:1–7](https://ref.ly/2Cor9:1-2Cor9:7). • and to offer sacrifices to God: See [Acts 21:23–26](https://ref.ly/Acts21:23-Acts21:26).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:17
* **Associated Articles:** Acts 20:1-2 (ID: `9463@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

## Acts 24:22 (id: 9525)

Perhaps Felix delayed his decision hoping that Paul would bribe him ([24:26](https://ref.ly/Acts24:26)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:22

## Acts 24:23 (id: 9526)

It was in keeping with the proper treatment of a Roman citizen to give him some freedom and allow his friends to visit him and take care of his needs.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Serve (ID: `keyterm:Serve`)
## Acts 24:24 (id: 9527)

Drusilla was the sister of Herod Agrippa II and Bernice ([25:13](https://ref.ly/Acts25:13)); Drusilla had abandoned her former husband, Azizus the King of Emessa, and married Felix. Drusilla was Jewish*,* so in forsaking her original husband and marrying Felix she had disregarded God’s commands ([Exod 20:14](https://ref.ly/Exod20:14); [Deut 5:18](https://ref.ly/Deut5:18); cp. [Mal 2:16](https://ref.ly/Mal2:16); [Mark 10:12](https://ref.ly/Mark10:12)). Josephus criticized her for this (*Antiquities* 20\.7\.1–2\). Drusilla and her husband were confronted in their conversation with Paul by the prospect of judgment.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Drusilla (ID: `person:Drusilla`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`)
## Acts 24:25 (id: 9528)

Paul’s words about righteousness*,* self\-control*,* and coming divine judgment frightened Felix*,* who was notably corrupt ([24:26–27](https://ref.ly/Acts24:26-Acts24:27); see study notes on 23:24; 24:24).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:25
* **Associated Articles:** Acts 23:24 (ID: `9509@TyndaleStudyNotes`); Acts 24:24 (ID: `9527@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

## Acts 24:26–27 (id: 9529)

Felix kept Paul in custody hoping for a bribe. When this failed and his own term ended, he left Paul in prison to gain favor with the Jewish people.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:26-27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Festus (ID: `person:Festus`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Hope (ID: `keyterm:Hope`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`)
## Acts 24:27–25.5 (id: 9530)

Felix’s successor was Porcius Festus*,* appointed by Nero to be governor of Judea about AD 59\~62\. Josephus describes Festus as a conscientious, honest administrator who was not able to stem the rising tide of Jewish unrest despite his strong action against the party of the Assassins (Josephus, *Antiquities* 20\.8\.10; cp. [Acts 21:38](https://ref.ly/Acts21:38)). He resisted the Jewish leaders’ attempt to have Paul’s trial moved to Jerusalem, but he was not immune to their pressure ([25:9](https://ref.ly/Acts25:9)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 24:27-25:5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Festus (ID: `person:Festus`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Caesarea (ID: `place:Caesarea`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Ask for (ID: `keyterm:AskFor`)
## Acts 25:7 (id: 9540)

The serious accusations were probably those raised previously ([21:27–28](https://ref.ly/Acts21:27-Acts21:28); [24:5–9](https://ref.ly/Acts24:5-Acts24:9)), but the accusations were unsupported by the evidence ([24:10–13](https://ref.ly/Acts24:10-Acts24:13)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`)
## Acts 25:9 (id: 9541)

Festus, wanting to please the Jews: The governor would later state his official reason for delaying Paul’s trial and asking to move it to Jerusalem ([25:17–20](https://ref.ly/Acts25:17-Acts25:20)). His request was driven by politics, however, not justice.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Festus (ID: `person:Festus`); Favor (ID: `keyterm:Favor`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`)
## Acts 25:10–11 (id: 9533)

Festus’s suggestion that Paul be tried in Jerusalem motivated the apostle’s appeal to Caesar. Paul was not afraid of death, but he objected to being turned over to a biased court intent on murder, not justice ([25:7](https://ref.ly/Acts25:7)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:10-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Act Wickedly (ID: `keyterm:ActWickedly`); Claudius (ID: `person:Claudius`); Call Upon (ID: `keyterm:CallUpon`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`); Judgment Seat (ID: `realia:JudgmentSeat`)
## Acts 25:12 (id: 9534)

Festus granted Paul’s appeal. This fulfilled Paul’s conviction that he must see Rome ([19:21](https://ref.ly/Acts19:21); see also [23:11](https://ref.ly/Acts23:11); [27:24](https://ref.ly/Acts27:24); [Rom 1:13–15](https://ref.ly/Rom1:13-Rom1:15); [15:22–29](https://ref.ly/Rom15:22-Rom15:29)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Call Upon (ID: `keyterm:CallUpon`); Festus (ID: `person:Festus`); Claudius (ID: `person:Claudius`)
## Acts 25:13–22 (id: 9535)

Festus discussed Paul’s case with Herod Agrippa II (ruled AD 50–100\), who had come to Caesarea to make a courtesy call on the new governor.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:13-22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Festus (ID: `person:Festus`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Agrippa (ID: `person:Agrippa`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Bernice (ID: `person:Bernice`); Augustus (ID: `person:Augustus`); Felix (ID: `person:Felix`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Call Upon (ID: `keyterm:CallUpon`); Caesarea (ID: `place:Caesarea`); Decision (ID: `keyterm:Decision`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`); Condemn (ID: `keyterm:Condemn`); Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`); Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Claudius (ID: `person:Claudius`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`); Judgment Seat (ID: `realia:JudgmentSeat`)
## Acts 25:16 (id: 9536)

It was a fundamental principle: Roman law did not convict people without a trial. They must be given an opportunity to confront their accusers and defend themselves (cp. [19:38–39](https://ref.ly/Acts19:38-Acts19:39)). This put Roman judicial procedure in a favorable light for readers of Acts.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:16

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rome (ID: `place:Rome`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`)
## Acts 25:17 (id: 9537)

I didn’t delay: In contrast with Felix, his predecessor ([24:22–27](https://ref.ly/Acts24:22-Acts24:27)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Judgment Seat (ID: `realia:JudgmentSeat`)
## Acts 25:18–20 (id: 9538)

These verses give Festus’s official reasons for his actions; see also [25:9](https://ref.ly/Acts25:9). There were no criminal accusations against Paul; the objections were about their religion and centered on Paul’s claim that Jesus . . . is alive.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:18-20

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`); Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`)
## Acts 25:23–27 (id: 9539)

Paul’s hearing before King Agrippa was accompanied by all the pomp and circumstance appropriate for an official royal visit. The main purpose of the hearing was for Agrippa to advise Festus on what he should write in the appeal to Caesar, for there was no clear charge against Paul, and Festus himself believed Paul had done nothing deserving death.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 25:23-27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Agrippa (ID: `person:Agrippa`); Festus (ID: `person:Festus`); Bernice (ID: `person:Bernice`); Call Upon (ID: `keyterm:CallUpon`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Augustus (ID: `person:Augustus`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Discern (ID: `keyterm:Discern`); Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`); Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`); Audience Hall (ID: `realia:AudienceHall`)
## Acts 26:1–23 (id: 9542)

In his eloquent defense before King Agrippa*,* Paul argued that his preaching was completely consistent with the Jewish faith. The defense begins with a courteous acknowledgement of Agrippa’s competence to hear the evidence ([26:2–3](https://ref.ly/Acts26:2-Acts26:3)), outlines the nature of Paul’s background, Jewish training, and membership in the Pharisees ([26:4–5](https://ref.ly/Acts26:4-Acts26:5)), and explains that the charges against him are merely for believing the fulfillment of Jewish hopes for the resurrection ([26:6–8](https://ref.ly/Acts26:6-Acts26:8)). Paul then tells the story of his conversion from strong opponent of Christianity ([26:9–11](https://ref.ly/Acts26:9-Acts26:11)) through a vision on the way to Damascus ([26:12–18](https://ref.ly/Acts26:12-Acts26:18); see [9:1–18](https://ref.ly/Acts9:1-Acts9:18)). His preaching was nothing more than obeying this divine vision ([26:19–20](https://ref.ly/Acts26:19-Acts26:20)). Even though he encountered violent opposition from his fellow Jews ([26:21](https://ref.ly/Acts26:21)), God protected him as he taught a message that the Jews should have embraced ([26:22–23](https://ref.ly/Acts26:22-Acts26:23)). This defense is a model for Christians put on trial for their faith (see [9:15](https://ref.ly/Acts9:15); [Luke 21:12–15](https://ref.ly/Luke21:12-Luke21:15)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:1-23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jews (ID: `group:Jews`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Agrippa (ID: `person:Agrippa`); Gentiles (ID: `group:Gentile`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Authority (ID: `keyterm:Authority.2`); Resurrection (ID: `keyterm:Resurrection`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Hope (ID: `keyterm:Hope`); Damascus (ID: `place:Damascus`); Decided (ID: `keyterm:Decided`); Rescue (ID: `keyterm:Rescue`); Promise (ID: `keyterm:Promise`); Blaspheme (ID: `keyterm:Blaspheme`); Consecration (ID: `keyterm:Consecration.2`); Serve (ID: `keyterm:Serve`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Nazareth (ID: `place:Nazareth`); Repent (ID: `keyterm:Repent`); Bear Witness (ID: `keyterm:BearWitness`); Satan (ID: `deity:Satan`); Life (ID: `keyterm:Life`); Happy (ID: `keyterm:Happy`); Heavenly (ID: `keyterm:Heavenly`); Witness (ID: `keyterm:Witness`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Judea (ID: `place:Judea`); Pharisees (ID: `group:Pharisee`); Declare (ID: `keyterm:Declare`); Name (ID: `keyterm:Name`); Priesthood (ID: `keyterm:Priesthood`); From Heaven (ID: `keyterm:FromHeaven`); Daily Life (ID: `keyterm:DailyLife`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`); Temple (ID: `realia:Temple`); Synagogue (ID: `realia:Synagogue`); Prison (ID: `realia:Prison`)

Throughout his defense in this trial for his life, Paul also clearly set out the conditions for receiving new life in Christ (also in [26:20](https://ref.ly/Acts26:20), [23](https://ref.ly/Acts26:23); see [Matt 10:19–20](https://ref.ly/Matt10:19-Matt10:20)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:17-18

## Acts 26:22–23 (id: 9545)

Paul stressed God’s protection as he carried out his witness (cp. [3:18](https://ref.ly/Acts3:18); [10:43](https://ref.ly/Acts10:43); [Luke 24:25–27](https://ref.ly/Luke24:25-Luke24:27), [44–47](https://ref.ly/Luke24:44-Luke24:47)). Paul then called on his hearers to believe his message, that Jesus is the Messiah who fulfills the promises of the Old Testament (see [Luke 24:27](https://ref.ly/Luke24:27), [44](https://ref.ly/Luke24:44)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:22-23

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Declare (ID: `keyterm:Declare`); Resurrection (ID: `keyterm:Resurrection`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Gentiles (ID: `group:Gentile`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`)
## Acts 26:24 (id: 9546)

Paul, you are insane: Festus, a Roman, thought all this talk about the prophets and resurrection was crazy (cp. [17:18](https://ref.ly/Acts17:18), [32](https://ref.ly/Acts17:32)), and he concluded that Paul must have driven himself mad with too much study.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:24

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Festus (ID: `person:Festus`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`)
## Acts 26:26 (id: 9547)

they were not done in a corner: The major events of the Christian faith were historical matters of public record that witnesses could attest as factually true. Agrippa could not invalidate Paul’s statements of fact.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:26

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Persuade (ID: `keyterm:Persuade`); Cornerstone (ID: `realia:Cornerstone`)
## Acts 26:27–28 (id: 9548)

Paul’s question put Agrippa in a bind: If he said he believed the prophets*,* he knew Paul would press home the Christian message; if not, he would offend the devout Jews in his audience. Agrippa knew that Paul wasn’t crazy and that Paul’s testimony about Jesus was historically sound ([26:26](https://ref.ly/Acts26:26)). So Agrippa evaded Paul’s question and refused to face the claims of Christ, alleging that the statement given by Paul was too brief for him to arrive at a responsible decision.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:27-28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Agrippa (ID: `person:Agrippa`); Christians (ID: `group:Christ`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Believe (ID: `keyterm:Believe`); Persuade (ID: `keyterm:Persuade`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`)
## Acts 26:28 (id: 9549)

“Do you think you can persuade me to become a Christian so quickly?”: This enigmatic remark might have been ironic, incredulous, scoffing, or brushing off Paul’s challenge. It also might have been a direct statement of Paul’s persuasiveness, or a direct statement about or genuine question of Paul’s intention. It seems best to take Agrippa’s reply as deliberately evasive: He didn’t want to admit that he believed the prophets ([26:27](https://ref.ly/Acts26:27)), for Paul had just made a strong case, and the next step would be to believe in Jesus as the promised Messiah to whom the prophets pointed. Agrippa didn’t want to take that step. On the other hand, he didn’t want to say that he didn’t believe the prophets, for that would alienate the Jewish subjects to whose loyalties he wanted to appeal. His non\-committal response underlines his discomfort with Paul’s testimony.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:28

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Christians (ID: `group:Christ`); Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); Agrippa (ID: `person:Agrippa`); Persuade (ID: `keyterm:Persuade`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`)
## Acts 26:29 (id: 9550)

Paul’s bold answer shows his quickness in repartee. He challenges Agrippa and his whole audience about the value of knowing Christ and making a personal commitment to him.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:29

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Paul (ID: `person:Paul`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Chain (ID: `realia:Chain`)
## Acts 26:31 (id: 9551)

The consensus of these rulers was that Paul had not done anything to deserve death or imprisonment*;* this verdict was given repeatedly by the Roman authorities that considered Paul’s case ([25:25](https://ref.ly/Acts25:25); see [Luke 23:4](https://ref.ly/Luke23:4), [15](https://ref.ly/Luke23:15), [22](https://ref.ly/Luke23:22)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:31

## Acts 26:32 (id: 9552)

He could have been set free: The legal verdict was clear ([26:31](https://ref.ly/Acts26:31)). But as a practical matter, if he hadn’t appealed to Caesar*,* Paul might not have been alive ([25:1–11](https://ref.ly/Acts25:1-Acts25:11)). As it was, he was fulfilling God’s purposes for him ([23:11](https://ref.ly/Acts23:11)).

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 26:32

## Acts 27:1–28.16 (id: 9553)

The vivid nautical language used throughout the account of Paul’s journey to Rome yields one of the best available accounts of an ancient sea voyage. • This is the last “we” section in Acts (see also [16:10–17](https://ref.ly/Acts16:10-Acts16:17); [20:5–15](https://ref.ly/Acts20:5-Acts20:15); [21:1–18](https://ref.ly/Acts21:1-Acts21:18)). During the two years of Paul’s imprisonment, Luke had probably done much of the research for his Gospel throughout Judea and Galilee. Here, as a member of Paul’s sailing party, he was an eyewitness participant in the danger at sea.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 27:1-28:16

## Acts 28:3–6 (id: 9587)

The locals of Malta understood justice as a personified power or deity carrying out judgment on a criminal. When nothing bad happened to Paul, the natives understood him as having power over snakes and concluded that he himself was a god (cp. [14:11–12](https://ref.ly/Acts14:11-Acts14:12)). In fact, Paul’s survival demonstrated God’s protection (cp. [Mark 16:17–18](https://ref.ly/Mark16:17-Mark16:18)).

## Acts 28:16 (id: 9579)

Paul was allowed to have his own . . . lodging*,* apparently in private facilities, though he was guarded by a soldier. Though Paul was traveling in chains, “the word of God cannot be chained” ([2 Tim 2:9](https://ref.ly/2Tim2:9)). Paul was possibly treated so well because of his social status or Roman citizenship (cp. [Acts 16:37–38](https://ref.ly/Acts16:37-Acts16:38); [22:25–28](https://ref.ly/Acts22:25-Acts22:28)).

## Acts 28:17–20 (id: 9580)

Conscious that the Good News was to be presented to the Jews first ([13:46](https://ref.ly/Acts13:46); [Rom 1:16](https://ref.ly/Rom1:16)) and concerned that the false charges against him might already have reached Rome, Paul summoned the local Jewish leaders and gave an account of his life and work. He insisted that he was guilty of no criminal offense, but strong Jewish opposition had made it necessary for him to appeal to the emperor. Paul had nothing against his own people*;* rather, he wanted to explain his great conviction that the Messiah they had been expecting had already come in the person of Jesus of Nazareth.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:17-20

## Acts 28:31 (id: 9588)

Despite being under house arrest ([28:16](https://ref.ly/Acts28:16)), Paul boldly proclaimed the Kingdom message. • And no one tried to stop him (Greek akōlutōs, “without hindrance”): This single word in Greek is the last word of the book of Acts and one of the keys to its meaning: God’s word cannot be chained, even when its messengers are ([2 Tim 2:9](https://ref.ly/2Tim2:9); see [Phil 1:12–14](https://ref.ly/Phil1:12-Phil1:14)). Acts is the story of an unhindered message of Good News, available to all people throughout the world, whether Jew, Gentile, proselyte, rich, or poor. The mission of proclaiming this message is accomplished in the power of the Spirit ([Acts 1:8](https://ref.ly/Acts1:8)); it embraces Jews ([3:1–5:42](https://ref.ly/Acts3:1-Acts5:42)), Samaritans ([8:1–25](https://ref.ly/Acts8:1-Acts8:25)), converts to Judaism ([2:11](https://ref.ly/Acts2:11); [13:43](https://ref.ly/Acts13:43)), “God\-fearers” ([8:26–40](https://ref.ly/Acts8:26-Acts8:40); [9:32–11:18](https://ref.ly/Acts9:32-Acts11:18)), and Gentiles ([13:1–28:28](https://ref.ly/Acts13:1-Acts28:28)). • Luke ends his account with Paul still under house arrest in Rome (about AD 60–62\). Paul was later released and traveled freely. According to tradition, Paul was imprisoned again in Rome about AD 64 and was martyred there during Nero’s persecution of believers.

* **Associated Passages:** Acts 28:31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Proclaim (ID: `keyterm:Proclaim`); Kingdom (ID: `keyterm:Kingdom`); Jesus (ID: `person:Jesus.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)
